<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> One-way liveness on <i>h</i> points accepts a word of binary relations when their ordered product is nonempty. For every <i>h</i> ≥ 2, it has a nondeterministic automaton with $`h+3`$ states and no left moves, whereas every equivalent <i>s</i>-state two-way deterministic automaton satisfies $`4(s+2)^2\ge2^{\lfloor(h-2)/31\rfloor}`$. Partial transition rules, stay moves, and nonaccepting infinite computations are allowed. The alphabets are finite and grow with <i>h</i>, so the result rules out an alphabet-independent polynomial state bound for deterministic two-way simulation, already for one-way nondeterministic sources.

- **claim-1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every integer $h\ge2$, the language $\mathsf{OWL}_h$ is recognized by a nondeterministic automaton with $h+3$ states using no left moves. Every equivalent deterministic two-way automaton with $s$ states satisfies $$\label{eq:main-bound} 4(s+2)^2\ge 2^{\lfloor(h-2)/31\rfloor}.$$ If an initially accepting configuration counts as acceptance, $s+2$ in <a href="#eq:main-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-bound">[eq:main-bound]</a> may be replaced by $s+1$.
  - Explanation: One-way liveness has a small nondeterministic source while every deterministic two-way equivalent obeys the exponential state lower bound. The acceptance convention controls the shift in the state parameter.
- **claim-2** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-no-polynomial)): There are no absolute constants $C,c>0$ such that every $n$-state two-way nondeterministic automaton over every finite alphabet has an equivalent two-way deterministic automaton with at most $Cn^c$ states.
  - Explanation: No polynomial state conversion with constants independent of the finite alphabet can cover all nondeterministic two-way sources.
- **claim-3** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-rank-basic)): For $a,b\in\mathcal D_{m}$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(ab)\leq \min\{\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(a),\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(b)\}, \qquad m-\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(a)\leq \|\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(a)\|.$$ The units of $\mathcal D_{m}$ are exactly its rank-$m$ diagrams, which are the permutation diagrams. The only rank-$m$ idempotent is $\mathbf 1_m$. Conjugation by a permutation diagram preserves rank. For every $J\subseteq[m]$, the diagrams supported in $J$ form a submonoid of $\mathcal D_{m}$.
  - Explanation: Diagram multiplication cannot increase transversal rank. Fixed transversal indices control the rank defect, and full-rank diagrams are precisely the units.
- **claim-4** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-corners)): Let $e\in\mathcal D_{m}$ be idempotent and let $r=\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(e)$.* 1. *There is a unital injective homomorphism $$R_e:e\mathcal D_{m}e\longrightarrow\mathcal D_{r}$$ satisfying $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(R_e(z))=\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(z)$ for all $z\in e\mathcal D_{m}e$.* 2. *The idempotent satisfies $$\|\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(e)\|\leq 2(m-r).$$* 3. *For every $J\subseteq[m]$ there is a set $J'\subseteq[r]$, depending only on $e$, $J$, and the numbering chosen for $R_e$, such that $\|J'\|\leq \|J\|$ and $$\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(R_e(eae))\subseteq J' \quad\text{for every $a\in\mathcal D_{m}$ supported in $J$}.$$
  - Explanation: An idempotent corner admits a smaller matching representation preserving rank, with uniform support control for a family of sandwiches.
- **claim-5** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-unit-lifts)): Let $S\subseteq\mathcal D_{m}$ be nonempty and closed under multiplication, and let $\phi:S\to M$ be an onto multiplicative map to a monoid $M$. Let $q\in M$ be idempotent. There is an idempotent $e\in S$ with $\phi(e)=q$. For every such $e$, the reduced monoid $R_e(eSe)$ has a unital surjection $$\phi_e:R_e(eSe)\longrightarrow qMq, \qquad \phi_e(R_e(z))=\phi(z).$$ If $e$ has minimum rank among all idempotents in $S$ mapping to $q$, then every element of $eSe$ mapping to a unit of $qMq$ reduces to a permutation diagram. In particular, every unit of $qMq$ has a permutation lift in $R_e(eSe)$, and that lift is a unit of the reduced monoid.
  - Explanation: Idempotent images have idempotent preimages. Choosing a minimum-rank preimage makes unit images lift to permutation diagrams in the reduced corner.
- **claim-6** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-relation-corner)): Let $P\in\mathcal R_{H}$ be idempotent, and let $F\subseteq H$ satisfy $I_{F}PI_{F}=I_{F}$. Put $i=I_{F}$ and $Q=PiP$. Then $Q$ is idempotent, $PQ=QP=Q$, and restriction is a unital isomorphism $$\theta:Q\mathcal R_{H}Q\longrightarrow\mathcal R_{F}, \qquad \theta(Z)=iZi.$$ Its inverse sends $A\in\mathcal R_{F}$ to $PAP=QAQ$.
  - Explanation: Restriction identifies a suitable relation corner with the full relation monoid on the selected subset.
- **claim-7** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-rank-loss)): Let $H$ be a finite set of size $h\ge2$, let $m\ge0$, and let $S\subseteq\mathcal D_{m}$ be a submonoid containing $\mathbf 1$. Suppose that $\phi:S\to\mathcal R_{H}$ is a unital surjection and that every permutation of $H$ has a lift in $S$ which is a permutation diagram. If $a\in S$ is idempotent and $$\phi(a)=I_{H}\cup\{(x,y)\},\qquad x,y\in H,\quad x\ne y,$$ then $$m-\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(a)\ge L(h).$$
  - Explanation: A single off-diagonal relation addition forces exponential rank loss when the map reaches the entire relation monoid and permutations have permutation-diagram lifts.
- **claim-8** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-divisor-degree)): Let $H$ be a finite set of size $h\ge2$. If an identity-containing submonoid $S\subseteq\mathcal D_{k}$ admits a unital surjection $\phi:S\to\mathcal R_{H}$, then $$k\ge L(h)=2^{\lfloor(h-2)/31\rfloor}.$$ No hypothesis about permutation lifts is required here.
  - Explanation: A unital surjection onto the full relation monoid forces exponential diagram degree, even without an initial permutation-lift assumption.
- **claim-9** (construction, [source](#stmt-thm-machine-diagrams)): For every $s$-state deterministic machine in this model, there is a map $$\eta:\Sigma\cup\{\vdash,\dashv\}\longrightarrow\mathcal D_{k}, \qquad k=4(s+1)^2,$$ with the following property. Designate ports $1,2$ on each of the two sides as test ports. For every word $w=a_1\cdots a_\ell$, the machine accepts $w$ if and only if the diagram $$\eta(\vdash)\eta(a_1)\cdots\eta(a_\ell)\eta(\dashv)$$ pairs a left test port with a right test port. Each symbol diagram is independent of the input length, the symbol’s position, and its neighboring symbols.* *If acceptance instead requires entry into an accepting state after a positive number of transitions, the same conclusion holds with $k=4(s+2)^2$.
  - Explanation: Each deterministic machine has local symbol matchings whose product detects acceptance through opposite-side test ports. Symbol matchings depend only on the scanned symbol.
- **claim-10** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-sink-tree)): Let $V$ be finite, let $\sigma:V\rightharpoonup V$ be a partial function, and let $t\in V$ have no successor. Make an undirected multigraph on $V$ with one edge for each defined successor $v\mapsto\sigma(v)$, retaining self-loops and distinct edges with the same endpoints. Then a vertex lies in the connected component of $t$ if and only if some finite sequence of successor steps takes it to $t$. This component is a tree.
  - Explanation: The component of a terminal sink in a finite partial-successor graph consists exactly of vertices reaching that sink and is a tree.
- **claim-11** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-tree-tour)): For a finite tree with at least one edge, this rule has a single cycle on its directed edge traversals, for every choice of cyclic orders. If the two joins at two distinct leaves are opened, the resulting two paths each connect a tip at one leaf to a tip at the other.
  - Explanation: Cyclic incidence orders give a single tour of a finite nonempty tree. Opening joins at distinct leaves gives paths between those leaves.
- **claim-12** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-small-nfa)): For $h\ge2$, the language $\mathsf{OWL}_h$ has a nondeterministic automaton with $h+3$ states, with no left moves.
  - Explanation: Path states track a relation path, while auxiliary states handle initialization, acceptance, and rejection without left moves.
- **claim-13** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-syntactic-quotient)): Let $H$ be a nonempty finite set. Suppose a deterministic automaton recognizing nonzero relation products over $\mathcal R_{H}$ has a symbol-diagram representation in $\mathcal D_{k}$ as in Theorem <a href="#thm:machine-diagrams" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:machine-diagrams">9</a>. Let $S\subseteq\mathcal D_{k}$ be the submonoid generated by the ordinary-symbol diagrams, including the empty product. Then there is a unital surjection $S\to\mathcal R_{H}$ sending each ordinary-symbol diagram to its relation letter.
  - Explanation: Singleton-identity context letters distinguish different relation products, forcing a well-defined unital surjection from generated symbol diagrams.

```json
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-rank-basic",
        "exact_quote": "For $a,b\\in\\mathcal D_{m}$, $$\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}(ab)\\leq \\min\\{\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}(a),\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}(b)\\},\n  \\qquad\n  m-\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}(a)\\leq |\\mathop{\\mathrm{supp}}(a)|.$$ The units of $\\mathcal D_{m}$ are exactly its rank-$m$ diagrams, which are the permutation diagrams.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-corners",
        "exact_quote": "Let $e\\in\\mathcal D_{m}$ be idempotent and let $r=\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}(e)$.*\n\n1.  *There is a unital injective homomorphism $$R_e:e\\mathcal D_{m}e\\longrightarrow\\mathcal D_{r}$$ satisfying $\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}(R_e(z))=\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}(z)$ for all $z\\in e\\mathcal D_{m}e$.*\n\n2.  *The idempotent satisfies $$|\\mathop{\\mathrm{supp}}(e)|\\leq 2(m-r).$$*\n\n3.  *For every $J\\subseteq[m]$ there is a set $J'\\subseteq[r]$, depending only on $e$, $J$, and the numbering chosen for $R_e$, such that $|J'|\\leq |J|$ and $$\\mathop{\\mathrm{supp}}(R_e(eae))\\subseteq J'\n        \\quad\\text{for every $a\\in\\mathcal D_{m}$ supported in $J$}.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-129.2"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-corners",

          "source_id": "PM-129.2"
        }

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-unit-lifts",
        "exact_quote": "Let $S\\subseteq\\mathcal D_{m}$ be nonempty and closed under multiplication, and let $\\phi:S\\to M$ be an onto multiplicative map to a monoid $M$. Let $q\\in M$ be idempotent. There is an idempotent $e\\in S$ with $\\phi(e)=q$. For every such $e$, the reduced monoid $R_e(eSe)$ has a unital surjection $$\\phi_e:R_e(eSe)\\longrightarrow qMq,\n  \\qquad \\phi_e(R_e(z))=\\phi(z).$$ If $e$ has minimum rank among all idempotents in $S$ mapping to $q$, then every element of $eSe$ mapping to a unit of $qMq$ reduces to a permutation diagram. In particular, every unit of $qMq$ has a permutation lift in $R_e(eSe)$, and that lift is a unit of the reduced monoid.",
        "source_id": "PM-129.2"
      },

        "anchor": "stmt-thm-machine-diagrams",
        "exact_quote": "For every $s$-state deterministic machine in this model, there is a map $$\\eta:\\Sigma\\cup\\{\\vdash,\\dashv\\}\\longrightarrow\\mathcal D_{k},\n \\qquad k=4(s+1)^2,$$ with the following property. Designate ports $1,2$ on each of the two sides as test ports. For every word $w=a_1\\cdots a_\\ell$, the machine accepts $w$ if and only if the diagram $$\\eta(\\vdash)\\eta(a_1)\\cdots\\eta(a_\\ell)\\eta(\\dashv)$$ pairs a left test port with a right test port.         "exact_quote": "Let $V$ be finite, let $\\sigma:V\\rightharpoonup V$ be a partial function, and let $t\\in V$ have no successor. Make an undirected multigraph on $V$ with one edge for each defined successor $v\\mapsto\\sigma(v)$, retaining self-loops and distinct edges with the same endpoints. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
```
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Relation monoid** ([source](#pm-h-3)): For a finite set $H$, let $\mathcal R_{H}$ be the monoid of all binary relations on $H$.
- **Path-order relation product** ([source](#pm-h-3)): Products are in path order from left to right: $$(x,z)\in AB \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \text{there exists }y\in H\text{ with }(x,y)\in A,\ (y,z)\in B.$$
- **Subset identity** ([source](#pm-h-3)): Write $I_{F}=\{(x,x):x\in F\}$ for $F\subseteq H$; the identity is $I_{H}$ and the zero is the empty relation $0$.
- **One-way liveness** ([source](#pm-h-3)): For $H=[h]$ define $$\label{eq:liveness-language} \mathsf{OWL}_h=\{R_1\cdots R_\ell\in(\mathcal R_{[h]})^*: R_1\cdots R_\ell\ne0\}.$$
- **Empty relation-word product** ([source](#eq:liveness-language)): The product of the empty word is $I_{[h]}$, so $\varepsilon\in\mathsf{OWL}_h$.
- **Matching diagram** ([source](#pm-h-7)): A *matching diagram of degree $m$* is a perfect matching on two disjoint sets of ports, a left set and a right set, each indexed by $[m]$.
- **Transversal and cap** ([source](#pm-h-7)): A pair joining opposite sides is a *transversal*; a pair on one side is a *cap*.
- **Rank and support** ([source](#pm-h-7)): Write $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(a)$ for the number of transversals of $a$, and define $$\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(a)=\{i\in[m]: \text{$a$ does not pair left $i$ with right $i$}\}.$$
- **Supported diagram** ([source](#pm-h-7)): We say that $a$ is *supported in* $J\subseteq[m]$ if $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(a)\subseteq J$.
- **Idempotent corner** ([source](#pm-h-8)): If $e^2=e$ in a monoid and $S$ is a subset closed under multiplication and containing $e$, its *corner* $$eSe=\{ese:s\in S\}$$ is a monoid with identity $e$.
- **Rank-loss function** ([source](#sec:rank)): For every integer $h\ge2$, set $$\label{eq:rank-loss-function} L(h)=2^{\lfloor(h-2)/31\rfloor}.$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **rank-support** [claim](#stmt-lem-rank-basic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rank-basic): Count disjoint transversal paths and fixed indices. -> Rank and support inequalities.
- **corner-reduction** [claim](#stmt-lem-corners) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-corners): Delete prescribed caps and use the idempotent connector to number retained ports. -> Injective rank-preserving corner representation with uniform support control.
- **unit-lifts** [claim](#stmt-lem-unit-lifts) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-unit-lifts): Take idempotent powers and use minimum rank above a fixed image idempotent. -> Permutation lifts of units in the minimum-rank corner.
- **relation-corner** [claim](#stmt-lem-relation-corner) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-relation-corner): Restrict the sandwich corner to the selected subset. -> Full relation monoid on the subset.
- **rank-amplification** [claim](#stmt-thm-rank-loss) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-rank-loss): Induct on the relation-set size using conjugates, a shared support budget, and smaller full relation corners. -> Exponential lower bound on rank loss.
- **divisor-degree** [claim](#stmt-cor-divisor-degree) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-divisor-degree): Reduce at a minimum-rank lift of the relation identity and apply rank loss. -> Exponential lower bound on ambient matching degree.
- **sink-tree** [claim](#stmt-lem-sink-tree) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-sink-tree): Identify the backward basin of a terminal sink and count successor edges. -> Tree component consisting of vertices reaching the sink.
- **tree-tour** [claim](#stmt-lem-tree-tour) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-tree-tour): Insert a removed leaf traversal into the smaller tree tour. -> Single circular tour and leaf-to-leaf paths after opening joins.
- **local-diagrams** [claim](#stmt-thm-machine-diagrams) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-tree-tour-2): Normalize acceptance to a sink, supply candidate slots with separate unused leaves, and contract local wiring. -> Symbol-local matching representation of acceptance.
- **small-source** [claim](#stmt-prop-small-nfa) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-small-nfa): Nondeterministically follow relation edges through path states. -> Small no-left-move nondeterministic recognizer.
- **syntactic-quotient** [claim](#stmt-lem-syntactic-quotient) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-syntactic-quotient): Use singleton-identity context letters to distinguish unequal relation products. -> Unital surjection onto the full relation monoid.
- **main-bound** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-syntactic-quotient-2): Compare the machine representation degree with the relation-divisor lower bound. -> The deterministic lower bound under both acceptance conventions.
- **no-polynomial** [claim](#stmt-cor-no-polynomial) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-no-polynomial): Compare the exponential family lower bound against any fixed polynomial. -> No alphabet-independent polynomial state simulation.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **relation_path_liveness**: Acceptance is equivalent to the existence of a path through the ordered relation letters, including the empty word.
- **state_cost**: The source uses $h+3$ states with no left moves; every equivalent deterministic two-way automaton obeys the stated exponential state bound.
- **symbol_locality**: Each symbol matching depends only on its scanned symbol, and acceptance is read by joining opposite test sides.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **fixed_alphabet**: Infer the same separation for a fixed alphabet. -> The witness alphabet grows with the point set.
- **other_costs**: Treat the state lower bound as a uniform space or transition-table lower bound. -> State succinctness alone does not establish these different resource bounds.
- **extra_memory**: Use the diagram wiring as additional simulator storage. -> The encoding would change the machine model.
- **partial_relation_image**: Apply the rank argument to only the generated relation additions. -> The smaller relation corners may lack the arbitrary lifts used by induction.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The source automaton gives a finite construction for recognizing nonempty ordered relation products. The executable checks its relation-path semantics; it does not certify the exponential state lower bound or the local matching representation.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON list of cases with h and words of h-by-h relation bitmasks.
# PRECONDITIONS: 2 <= h <= 3; word length <= 3; integer masks in [0, 2**(h*h)).
# OUTPUTS: JSON list of booleans for nonempty ordered relation products.
# TIME: O(sum((1 + word_length)*h*h)) exact operations.
# SPACE: O(h + number_of_cases) auxiliary entries, apart from input storage.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact Python integers and sets; no rounding or solver witnesses.
# FAILURE: Invalid input raises ValueError; no claim about arbitrary machines.
import json, sys

def live(case):
    h, word = case['h'], case['word']
    if type(h) is not int or not 2 <= h <= 3 or not isinstance(word, list) or len(word) > 3:
        raise ValueError('outside finite contract')
    if any(type(a) is not int or not 0 <= a < 2**(h*h) for a in word):
        raise ValueError('invalid relation')
    active = set(range(h))
    for mask in word:
        active = {q for p in active for q in range(h) if (mask >> (p*h+q)) & 1}
    return bool(active)

cases = json.load(sys.stdin)
print(json.dumps([live(case) for case in cases]))
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | A finite-state recognizer for nonempty ordered relation products can follow the explicit nondeterministic construction. The deterministic simulation obstruction applies only when the software recognizer satisfies the stated automaton model. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A game or simulation contract is ABSENT. The source treats nondeterministic branches as existential accepting computations and supplies no strategic interaction or simulation fidelity guarantee. |
| tools | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | The explicit liveness recognizer and singleton-identity context tests can inform a specialized relation-product checking tool. A general-purpose analysis tool and its resource guarantees are ABSENT. |
| algorithms | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The paper provides finite constructions for recognizing one-way liveness, multiplying matching diagrams, and touring a sink tree. Its exponential result is a state lower bound for deterministic recognition, not a running-time lower bound for these constructions. |
| data_structures | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Perfect matchings and explicit incidence orders give concrete representations for relation and diagram computations. Crossings have no semantic role, and discarded internal components must not alter outside connectivity. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | An accelerator design or hardware resource contract is ABSENT. Auxiliary wires encode a computation graph algebraically and do not enlarge the simulator memory. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A cryptographic primitive, threat model, or security reduction is ABSENT. The obstruction concerns equivalent finite-state recognizers. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A learning procedure, training objective, or statistical guarantee is ABSENT. The recognition model excludes randomness and stores control in a finite state. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | An optimisation objective or operations-research method is ABSENT. Minimum-rank corner choices support the algebraic existence proof without supplying an optimisation resource bound. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A distributed protocol contract is ABSENT. The model has a read-only head and finite control, with no additional heads or writable cells. |
| other (Automata state succinctness and finite monoid theory) | True | strong | inspirational | stated | Automata state succinctness and finite monoid representations are the direct applications. A local matching representation supplies a quadratic diagram degree, while a unital surjection onto the entire relation monoid forces the exponential degree obstruction. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-alphabet** (counterexample_scope): The separation concerns growing finite alphabets and does not establish a separate fixed-alphabet lower bound. Blocked use: ['A fixed-alphabet exponential state lower bound.']
- **limit-resource** (formal_scope): The result measures finite-state succinctness; it supplies neither a uniform logarithmic-space separation nor a transition-table-size lower bound. Blocked use: ['A uniform logarithmic-space separation.', 'A lower bound measured by transition-table size.']
- **limit-machine** (hypothesis): The deterministic simulator has one read-only head and finite control under the stated model. The lower bound does not establish the corresponding claim for richer machine models. Blocked use: ['Transfer of the state lower bound to machines with extra heads, writable cells, work tapes, advice, or randomness.']
- **limit-termination** (edge_case): Acceptance requires a finite accepting computation; a nonaccepting infinite computation rejects. Infinite computations therefore cannot be discarded by assuming every target halts. Blocked use: ['Treating nontermination as acceptance.', 'Replacing the target model by a halting model without justification.']
- **limit-initial** (edge_case): The accepting-initial-configuration convention and the positive-transition convention require different state normalization in the local matching representation. Blocked use: ['Using the smaller diagram-degree expression under the positive-transition convention without its additional initial copy.']
- **limit-empty** (edge_case): The empty relation word is live because its product is the identity relation; an implementation must preserve that convention. Blocked use: ['Initializing the empty product as the empty relation.', 'Rejecting the empty word in the stated liveness language.']
- **limit-surjection** (hypothesis): The rank-loss proof requires a unital surjection onto the entire relation monoid; representing only the displayed additions does not satisfy this precondition. Blocked use: ['Applying the rank-loss amplification to an image consisting only of the generated additions.']
- **limit-lifts** (hypothesis): The rank-loss theorem assumes permutation-diagram lifts; the divisor-degree corollary removes that assumption by passing to a minimum-rank idempotent corner. Blocked use: ['Omitting permutation lifts when directly invoking the rank-loss theorem.']
- **limit-tree** (hypothesis): The tour lemma applies to a finite tree with an edge; determinism establishes a tree only for the component reaching the designated sink. Blocked use: ['Applying the single-cycle tour assertion to arbitrary cyclic components.', 'Applying the tour lemma to an edgeless tree.']
- **limit-corner** (hypothesis): Restriction identifies a full smaller relation corner only under the stated idempotence and identity-restriction hypotheses. Blocked use: ['Restricting an arbitrary relation sandwich and claiming a unital isomorphism.']
- **limit-time** (formal_scope): The state separation imposes no input-length or running-time bound and does not provide an execution-time guarantee. Blocked use: ['A runtime lower bound or performance guarantee inferred from state succinctness.']
- **limit-encoding** (formal_scope): Auxiliary graph vertices and wires belong to the algebraic representation rather than the simulator memory. Blocked use: ['Counting the encoding as extra simulator memory.', 'Granting the simulator auxiliary graph storage under the stated theorem.']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 129 (`lean/docs/129.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [An exponential state lower bound for two-way nondeterministic complementation](../../preprints/An-exponential-state-lower-bound-for-two-way-nondeterministic-complementation-September-25-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [An exponential two-way deterministic state lower bound for one-way liveness](../../preprints/An-exponential-two-way-deterministic-state-lower-bound-for-one-way-liveness-September-25-2026/main.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The paper asks how many states are needed to complement or determinize two-way nondeterministic finite automata. The formalization gives, for every $n\ge4$, an explicit $n$-state automaton whose complement requires at least $\tfrac12 2^{\lfloor(n-4)/127\rfloor}-1$ states. For the same family of relational languages and every $n\ge131$, every equivalent deterministic two-way automaton requires at least $\tfrac12 2^{\lfloor(n-4)/127\rfloor}$ states.
>
> The alphabet grows with $n$, so neither complementation nor determinization has a polynomial state bound uniform over alphabets. The model has two endmarkers, left, right, and stay moves, and acceptance by a finite run. The separate results about one-way liveness and the Sakoda–Sipser problem are outside this scope.
>
> The formalization gives a family of one-way nondeterministic automata with $h+3$ states whose languages require exponentially many states for two-way deterministic recognition. For every $h\ge2$, a deterministic recognizer with $s$ states satisfies $2^{\lfloor(h-2)/31\rfloor}\le4(s+2)^2$ under both acceptance conventions considered in the paper; one convention has the sharper factor $4(s+1)^2$. The alphabet may grow with $h$, so no alphabet-independent polynomial simulation bound holds.

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace OneWayLiveness

@[ext] structure BRel (H : Type*) where
  holds : H → H → Prop

namespace BRel
variable {H : Type*}
instance : One (BRel H) := ⟨⟨Eq⟩⟩
instance : Mul (BRel H) := ⟨fun a b => ⟨fun x z => ∃ y, a.holds x y ∧ b.holds y z⟩⟩
instance : Monoid (BRel H) where
  mul_assoc a b c := by
    ext x z
    change (∃ y, (∃ w, a.holds x w ∧ b.holds w y) ∧ c.holds y z) ↔
      ∃ w, a.holds x w ∧ ∃ y, b.holds w y ∧ c.holds y z
    aesop
  one_mul a := by ext x z; change (∃ y, x = y ∧ a.holds y z) ↔ a.holds x z; simp
  mul_one a := by ext x z; change (∃ y, a.holds x y ∧ y = z) ↔ a.holds x z; simp

/-- Nonemptiness is the existence of a path through the word's ordered product. -/
def live (w : List (BRel H)) : Prop := ∃ x y, w.prod.holds x y
end BRel

abbrev Alphabet (h : ℕ) := BRel (Fin h)
def OWL (h : ℕ) : Set (List (Alphabet h)) := {w | BRel.live w}

inductive Symbol (Alpha : Type*) where
  | left : Symbol Alpha
  | letter : Alpha → Symbol Alpha
  | right : Symbol Alpha

inductive Move where
  | left | stay | right
  deriving DecidableEq

def Move.Rel : Move → ℕ → ℕ → Prop
  | .left, i, j => j + 1 = i
  | .stay, i, j => j = i
  | .right, i, j => j = i + 1

/-- The finite word including both endmarkers. -/
def scanned {Alpha : Type*} (w : List Alpha) (i : Fin (w.length + 2)) : Symbol Alpha :=
  if h0 : i.val = 0 then .left
  else if hi : i.val < w.length + 1 then .letter (w[i.val - 1]'(by omega))
  else .right

abbrev Configuration {Alpha : Type*} (w : List Alpha) (n : ℕ) := Fin (w.length + 2) × Fin n

structure DMachine (Alpha : Type*) (s : ℕ) where
  initial : Fin s
  accepting : Set (Fin s)
  transition : Fin s → Symbol Alpha → Option (Fin s × Move)
  left_boundary : ∀ q q', transition q .left ≠ some (q', .left)
  right_boundary : ∀ q q', transition q .right ≠ some (q', .right)

structure NMachine (Alpha : Type*) (n : ℕ) where
  initial : Fin n
  accepting : Set (Fin n)
  transition : Fin n → Symbol Alpha → Set (Fin n × Move)
  left_boundary : ∀ q q', (q', .left) ∉ transition q .left
  right_boundary : ∀ q q', (q', .right) ∉ transition q .right

def DMachine.Step {Alpha : Type*} {s : ℕ} (M : DMachine Alpha s) (w : List Alpha)
    (c c' : Configuration w s) : Prop :=
  ∃ d, M.transition c.2 (scanned w c.1) = some (c'.2, d) ∧ d.Rel c.1.val c'.1.val

def NMachine.Step {Alpha : Type*} {n : ℕ} (M : NMachine Alpha n) (w : List Alpha)
    (c c' : Configuration w n) : Prop :=
  ∃ d, (c'.2, d) ∈ M.transition c.2 (scanned w c.1) ∧ d.Rel c.1.val c'.1.val

/-- The positive convention explicitly excludes a length-zero computation. -/
def FiniteRun {C : Type*} (positive : Bool) (step : C → C → Prop) (c c' : C) : Prop :=
  if positive then Relation.TransGen step c c' else Relation.ReflTransGen step c c'

def DMachine.Accepts {Alpha : Type*} {s : ℕ} (M : DMachine Alpha s)
    (positive : Bool) (w : List Alpha) : Prop :=
  ∃ c, FiniteRun positive (M.Step w) (⟨0, by omega⟩, M.initial) c ∧ c.2 ∈ M.accepting

def NMachine.Accepts {Alpha : Type*} {n : ℕ} (M : NMachine Alpha n)
    (positive : Bool) (w : List Alpha) : Prop :=
  ∃ c, FiniteRun positive (M.Step w) (⟨0, by omega⟩, M.initial) c ∧ c.2 ∈ M.accepting

def NMachine.NoLeft {Alpha : Type*} {n : ℕ} (M : NMachine Alpha n) : Prop :=
  ∀ q a q', (q', Move.left) ∉ M.transition q a

def NMachine.Recognizes {Alpha : Type*} {n : ℕ} (M : NMachine Alpha n)
    (positive : Bool) (L : Set (List Alpha)) : Prop := ∀ w, M.Accepts positive w ↔ w ∈ L

def DMachine.Recognizes {Alpha : Type*} {s : ℕ} (M : DMachine Alpha s)
    (positive : Bool) (L : Set (List Alpha)) : Prop := ∀ w, M.Accepts positive w ↔ w ∈ L

theorem main_theorem (h : ℕ) (hh : 2 ≤ h) :
    (∃ N : NMachine (Alphabet h) (h + 3), N.NoLeft ∧
      ∀ positive : Bool, N.Recognizes positive (OWL h)) ∧
    (∀ (positive : Bool) (s : ℕ) (D : DMachine (Alphabet h) s),
      D.Recognizes positive (OWL h) →
      2 ^ ((h - 2) / 31) ≤ 4 * (s + if positive then 2 else 1) ^ 2) := by
  sorry

end OneWayLiveness

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


One-way liveness on <i>h</i> points accepts a word of binary relations when their ordered product is nonempty. For every <i>h</i> ≥ 2, it has a nondeterministic automaton with $`h+3`$ states and no left moves, whereas every equivalent <i>s</i>-state two-way deterministic automaton satisfies $`4(s+2)^2\ge2^{\lfloor(h-2)/31\rfloor}`$. Partial transition rules, stay moves, and nonaccepting infinite computations are allowed. The alphabets are finite and grow with <i>h</i>, so the result rules out an alphabet-independent polynomial state bound for deterministic two-way simulation, already for one-way nondeterministic sources.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

Two-way deterministic automata recognize the same languages as one-way finite automata, as established by Rabin and Scott and by Shepherdson (Rabin and Scott 1959; Shepherdson 1959) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[RabinScott1959](#ref-RabinScott1959), [Shepherdson1959](#ref-Shepherdson1959)]. Allowing the head to return to a symbol can nevertheless change the amount of finite control needed to recognize a language. How much finite control is needed to replace nondeterministic choices by deterministic head motion? Sakoda and Sipser posed the state cost of converting one-way and two-way nondeterministic finite automata to two-way deterministic automata in 1978 (Sakoda and Sipser 1978) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[SakodaSipser1978](#ref-SakodaSipser1978)]. The question concerns succinctness: the simulator may revisit the input, but must store all information other than the head position in its finite state. A polynomial simulation would make nondeterminism inexpensive in this measure even when the source machine itself moves in both directions.

The witness used here is Sakoda and Sipser’s complete family $B_h$, now called *one-way liveness*: each letter specifies a binary relation on an $h$-element set, and a word is live when the product of its letters is nonempty. Their Theorem 2.3 establishes this family’s completeness for one-way-nondeterministic to two-way-deterministic state conversion (Sakoda and Sipser 1978) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[SakodaSipser1978](#ref-SakodaSipser1978)]. Our proof below gives the small source automaton and the context-separation property directly, with the conventions and exact state counts used here.

Earlier exponential lower bounds restrict the motion of the deterministic target. Sipser proved an exponential separation for sweeping automata, whose head changes direction only at an endmarker (Sipser 1980b) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Sipser1980](#ref-Sipser1980)]. Kapoutsis extended this phenomenon to deterministic machines constrained to make sublinearly many reversals as a function of input length (C. Kapoutsis 2013) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Kapoutsis2013](#ref-Kapoutsis2013)]. An unrestricted target can instead reverse at an interior cell and make arbitrarily many reversals as the input length grows.

Quadratic lower bounds against unrestricted two-way deterministic targets already occur in Chrobak’s work on unary languages (Chrobak 1986, 2003) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Chrobak1986Unary](#ref-Chrobak1986Unary), [Chrobak2003Errata](#ref-Chrobak2003Errata)]. For the promise restriction of one-way liveness to words of exactly three relation letters, Kapoutsis proved the optimal order $\Theta(h^2/\log h)$ (C. A. Kapoutsis 2018) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Kapoutsis2018ShortLiveness](#ref-Kapoutsis2018ShortLiveness)]; the alphabet there is still the full relation alphabet. Adeogun and Kapoutsis (Adeogun and Kapoutsis 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[AdeogunKapoutsis2026](#ref-AdeogunKapoutsis2026)] prove a quadratic lower bound for unrestricted deterministic two-way automata against one-way liveness. We prove an exponential bound for that unrestricted target model. The source automata use no left moves, whereas the lower bound permits stay moves, partial transition rules, and nonaccepting infinite computations in the target.

The main representation difficulty is to account for repeated visits to a cell while assigning each input symbol a fixed object independent of its neighbors. We use the Brauer diagram monoid, whose matchings underlie Brauer’s algebras (Brauer 1937; Auinger 2012) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Brauer1937](#ref-Brauer1937), [Auinger2012](#ref-Auinger2012)]: its elements are perfect matchings on two equally sized sets of ports, and multiplication glues the adjoining sets and contracts paths. Determinism makes the configurations reaching a designated accepting sink form a tree. A tour of that tree gives the required matching representation, with separate leaves for unused transition candidates ensuring that each cell’s wiring depends only on its own symbol. The algebraic part then proves an exponential lower bound on the number of ports whenever matching products retain all the information in arbitrary relation products. All diagram and relation-monoid facts needed for this argument are proved below.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Model and statement

An automaton has a finite state set and one read-only head on a finite word over an alphabet $\Sigma$, bracketed by two distinct endmarkers outside $\Sigma$. Its head starts at the left endmarker in a designated initial state. A transition changes the state and moves the head left, right, or not at all; the head cannot cross an endmarker. A transition depends only on the current state and scanned symbol. Deterministic transition rules may be partial. A nondeterministic rule permits several successors. There are no additional heads, writable cells, work tapes, advice, or randomness. All states, including initial and accepting states, are counted.

Acceptance means that a finite computation reaches an accepting state; a nonaccepting infinite computation rejects. We initially count an accepting initial configuration as success. If success instead requires a positive transition into an accepting state, the normalization in Section <a href="#sec:machines" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:machines">4</a> uses one additional initial copy. The main bound below includes this extra state and holds with either convention.

For a finite set $H$, let $\mathcal R_{H}$ be the monoid of all binary relations on $H$. Products are in path order from left to right: $$(x,z)\in AB
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \text{there exists }y\in H\text{ with }(x,y)\in A,\ (y,z)\in B.$$ Write $I_{F}=\{(x,x):x\in F\}$ for $F\subseteq H$; the identity is $I_{H}$ and the zero is the empty relation $0$. For $H=[h]$ define <a id="eq:liveness-language"></a>$$\label{eq:liveness-language}
 \mathsf{OWL}_h=\{R_1\cdots R_\ell\in(\mathcal R_{[h]})^*:
                  R_1\cdots R_\ell\ne0\}.$$ The product of the empty word is $I_{[h]}$, so $\varepsilon\in\mathsf{OWL}_h$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:main-bound"></a>
**Theorem 1**. *For every integer $h\ge2$, the language $\mathsf{OWL}_h$ is recognized by a nondeterministic automaton with $h+3$ states using no left moves. Every equivalent deterministic two-way automaton with $s$ states satisfies $$\label{eq:main-bound}
 4(s+2)^2\ge 2^{\lfloor(h-2)/31\rfloor}.$$ If an initially accepting configuration counts as acceptance, $s+2$ in <a href="#eq:main-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-bound">[eq:main-bound]</a> may be replaced by $s+1$.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-cor-no-polynomial"></a>
<div id="cor:no-polynomial" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2**. *There are no absolute constants $C,c>0$ such that every $n$-state two-way nondeterministic automaton over every finite alphabet has an equivalent two-way deterministic automaton with at most $Cn^c$ states.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-no-polynomial"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-no-polynomial</summary>

*Proof.* Put $n=h+3$ in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Every deterministic equivalent for this family has at least $$2^{\frac12\lfloor(n-5)/31\rfloor-1}-2$$ states. This bound exceeds $Cn^c$ for every fixed $C,c$ when $n$ is large enough. For each $h$ the alphabet has $2^{h^2}$ symbols and is finite. Since the constants in the proposed simulation must be independent of the alphabet, the family is within its scope. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof architecture

Write $\mathcal D_{k}$ for the matching monoid of degree $k$. The proof compares two bounds on $k$. Theorem <a href="#thm:machine-diagrams" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:machine-diagrams">9</a> represents each $s$-state deterministic machine by symbol diagrams in $\mathcal D_{k}$, with $k\le4(s+2)^2$. For a machine recognizing $\mathsf{OWL}_h$, Lemma <a href="#lem:syntactic-quotient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:syntactic-quotient">13</a> gives a unital surjection from the submonoid generated by its ordinary-symbol diagrams onto $\mathcal R_{[h]}$. Concretely, the map sends each word diagram to its relation product, preserves multiplication and the empty product, and reaches every relation on $[h]$. These three properties, including the full image, are the algebraic input to the lower bound. Corollary <a href="#cor:divisor-degree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:divisor-degree">8</a> then forces $k\ge2^{\lfloor(h-2)/31\rfloor}$. These three statements give the deterministic lower bound in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

We prove the algebraic bound first. The *rank* of a matching diagram counts its pairs joining opposite sides. A diagram $e$ is *idempotent* if $e^2=e$; its *corner* consists of the diagrams $z$ with $ez=ze=z$, with identity $e$. Section <a href="#sec:matching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:matching">2</a> reduces the corner of an idempotent of rank $r$ to degree $r$, preserving rank and controlling the indices at which the diagram differs from the identity. An idempotent of rank $r$ in $\mathcal D_{k}$ differs from the identity on at most $2(k-r)$ indices. The support control is uniform: a family supported in one set remains supported in a single set of no greater size after reduction. Section <a href="#sec:rank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rank">3</a> uses $32$ conjugates of one idempotent to make $256$ successive relation additions. A common support set bounds their total rank loss, while each addition yields a further corner representing all relations on $h-31$ points. Comparing these bounds doubles the required rank loss every $31$ points.

Section <a href="#sec:machines" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:machines">4</a> obtains local symbol diagrams by converting acceptance to reachability of a designated sink. Its undirected component is a tree, whose doubled tour supplies the matching test. This traversal has precedents in Sipser’s halting method and in the reversible simulation of Lange, McKenzie, and Tapp (Sipser 1980a; Lange, McKenzie, and Tapp 2000) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[SipserHalting1980](#ref-SipserHalting1980), [LangeMcKenzieTapp2000](#ref-LangeMcKenzieTapp2000)]. Candidate transition slots completed by separate leaves make the construction independent of neighboring symbols. Finally, Section <a href="#sec:separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:separation">5</a> obtains the quotient by using singleton-identity context letters to test each pair in a relation product. No input-length or running-time bound is imposed.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Related results

Adeogun and Kapoutsis also analyze limitations of their earlier lower-bound construction (Adeogun and Kapoutsis 2026b) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[AdeogunKapoutsisLimitation2026](#ref-AdeogunKapoutsisLimitation2026)]. They prove a quadratic ceiling for its sequences of connectivity properties and state a general-property extension whose proof is deferred. The amplification here uses a fixed chain of $256$ additions. Its exponential bound comes from the inductive lower bound on each rank loss in a smaller full relation corner, together with one common support budget for all additions.

The companion paper (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[CompanionComplementation2026](#ref-CompanionComplementation2026)] proves that a nondeterministic two-way automaton for the complement of $\mathsf{OWL}_h$ requires at least $\frac12 2^{\lfloor(h-2)/127\rfloor}-1$ states. The present proof establishes the deterministic bound directly using matching diagrams. Both results concern state succinctness over growing alphabets; neither yields a uniform logarithmic-space separation or a bound measured by transition-table size.

<a id="sec:matching"></a>
### Matching and relation corners

We first establish the algebraic properties used in the rank argument. All monoid products in this paper are in path order: in a product $ab$, the factor $a$ is placed to the left of the factor $b$. A homomorphism is called *unital* when it preserves the designated identities.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Matching diagrams, rank, and support

For an integer $m\geq 0$, let $[m]=\{1,\ldots,m\}$, with $[0]=\varnothing$. A *matching diagram of degree $m$* is a perfect matching on two disjoint sets of ports, a left set and a right set, each indexed by $[m]$. Only the paired ports matter; crossings in a drawing have no significance. Let $\mathcal D_{m}$ be the set of these diagrams.

To multiply $a,b\in\mathcal D_{m}$, identify the right port indexed $i$ of $a$ with the left port indexed $i$ of $b$, for every $i\in[m]$. In the resulting finite multigraph, each outside port has degree one and each identified port has degree two. Thus every component meeting an outside port is a path with two outside endpoints. Pair these endpoints and discard the components without outside ports. The result is $ab$. Parallel edges, when they occur at the interface, are counted separately.

This multiplication is associative. Indeed, in any string of glued diagrams, a substring can be replaced by the matching of its boundary endpoints: each path through the substring is replaced by one edge with the same endpoints. This preserves the connections between the outer ports of the whole string. Components internal to the substring have no connection to those ports. Contracting the first or the last two factors in a string of three therefore gives the same outside matching. The diagram $\mathbf 1_m$ pairing each left port $i$ with right port $i$ is an identity. In particular, $\mathcal D_{0}=\{\mathbf 1_0\}$ is the one-element monoid. The monoids $\mathcal D_{m}$ are the Brauer diagram monoids, whose matchings underlie Brauer’s algebras (Brauer 1937) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Brauer1937](#ref-Brauer1937)]. Their semigroup structure and transversal rank have been studied in particular by Auinger and by East and Gray (Auinger 2012; East and Gray 2017) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Auinger2012](#ref-Auinger2012), [EastGray2017](#ref-EastGray2017)].

A pair joining opposite sides is a *transversal*; a pair on one side is a *cap*. Write $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(a)$ for the number of transversals of $a$, and define $$\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(a)=\{i\in[m]:
      \text{$a$ does not pair left $i$ with right $i$}\}.$$ We say that $a$ is *supported in* $J\subseteq[m]$ if $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(a)\subseteq J$.

<a id="stmt-lem-rank-basic"></a>
<div id="lem:rank-basic" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Rank and support). *For $a,b\in\mathcal D_{m}$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(ab)\leq \min\{\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(a),\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(b)\},
  \qquad
  m-\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(a)\leq |\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(a)|.$$ The units of $\mathcal D_{m}$ are exactly its rank-$m$ diagrams, which are the permutation diagrams. The only rank-$m$ idempotent is $\mathbf 1_m$. Conjugation by a permutation diagram preserves rank. For every $J\subseteq[m]$, the diagrams supported in $J$ form a submonoid of $\mathcal D_{m}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rank-basic"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rank-basic</summary>

*Proof.* Each transversal path in the product uses a transversal of $a$ and a transversal of $b$. Different product paths are disjoint, so they use distinct transversals of either factor. This proves the rank inequality. Every index outside $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(a)$ supplies a fixed transversal, proving the support inequality.

A rank-$m$ diagram is a bijection between its two sides and has the inverse permutation diagram as a two-sided inverse. Conversely, the rank inequality forces every unit to have rank $m$. An idempotent in a group is its identity, proving the assertion about idempotents. If $u$ is a permutation diagram, the rank inequality gives $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(uau^{-1})\leq\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(a)$; applying it to $a=u^{-1}(uau^{-1})u$ gives the reverse inequality.

If both $a$ and $b$ fix the transversal at an index $i$, their product also fixes it. Hence any product of diagrams supported in $J$ is supported in $J$, and $\mathbf 1_m$ is supported in every such set. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Reduction at an idempotent

If $e^2=e$ in a monoid and $S$ is a subset closed under multiplication and containing $e$, its *corner* $$eSe=\{ese:s\in S\}$$ is a monoid with identity $e$. In particular, every $z\in eSe$ satisfies $ez=ze=z$. This is the standard local-monoid construction of finite-semigroup theory (Pin 1997, sec. 2.7) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Pin1997](#ref-Pin1997)]. For $m\ge3$, Auinger observes that the corner of the standard rank-$(m-2)$ projection in $\mathcal D_{m}$ is isomorphic to $\mathcal D_{m-2}$ (Auinger 2012, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Auinger2012](#ref-Auinger2012)]. The following reduction treats an arbitrary idempotent and realizes its matching corner on fewer ports. Its third part controls one common support set for an entire family of sandwiches.

<a id="stmt-lem-corners"></a>
<div id="lem:corners" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Matching corners). *Let $e\in\mathcal D_{m}$ be idempotent and let $r=\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(e)$.*

1.  *There is a unital injective homomorphism $$R_e:e\mathcal D_{m}e\longrightarrow\mathcal D_{r}$$ satisfying $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(R_e(z))=\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(z)$ for all $z\in e\mathcal D_{m}e$.*

2.  *The idempotent satisfies $$|\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(e)|\leq 2(m-r).$$*

3.  *For every $J\subseteq[m]$ there is a set $J'\subseteq[r]$, depending only on $e$, $J$, and the numbering chosen for $R_e$, such that $|J'|\leq |J|$ and $$\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(R_e(eae))\subseteq J'
        \quad\text{for every $a\in\mathcal D_{m}$ supported in $J$}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-corners"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-corners</summary>

*Proof.* Let $X,Y\subseteq[m]$ be the indices of the left and right transversal endpoints of $e$, respectively. Its transversals define a bijection $p:X\to Y$, with $|X|=|Y|=r$.

We describe the connector at the interface between two copies of $e$. Keep two distinct tagged rows of vertices, $R_i$ and $L_i$ for $i\in[m]$, and put an edge $R_iL_i$ between each matching pair of indices. On the $R$ row put the right caps of $e$, and on the $L$ row put the left caps of $e$. Thus the degree-one vertices are precisely $$\{R_y:y\in Y\}\ \cup\ \{L_x:x\in X\},$$ and all other vertices have degree two. These tagged vertices remain distinct even when $X$ and $Y$ overlap. Pairing the endpoints of its paths, and discarding its closed components, gives a matching on the $2r$ displayed vertices.

This is exactly the connector encountered by the transversals in $e\mathbf 1_m e=e^2$. Since $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(e^2)=r$, all $r$ left transversal endpoints of the first copy must reach the right boundary of the second. Consequently the connector cannot pair two $R$ endpoints or two $L$ endpoints: it pairs $R_y$ with $L_{\gamma(y)}$ for a bijection $\gamma:Y\to X$. The path from an outer left endpoint $x\in X$ ends at the right endpoint $p(\gamma(p(x)))$. Equality $e^2=e$ gives $$p(\gamma(p(x)))=p(x),
  \qquad\text{hence}\qquad \gamma(p(x))=x.$$ Thus $\gamma=p^{-1}$.

Every diagram $z\in e\mathcal D_{m}e$ contains the left caps of $e$ on its outer left side and the right caps of $e$ on its outer right side. Delete precisely these prescribed caps. The remaining ports are the left ports indexed by $X$ and the right ports indexed by $Y$, and they retain a perfect matching. In particular, any additional caps among these retained ports remain in the reduced diagram. Number $X$ by $[r]$ in any fixed way, and give the right endpoint $p(x)$ the same number as $x$. This defines $R_e(z)$.

Figure <a href="#fig:corner-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:corner-reduction">1</a> illustrates the connector and the numbering for a rank-$2$ idempotent.

<figure id="fig:corner-reduction">

<figcaption>A rank-$2$ idempotent of degree $4$ and its reduction. In the left panel, the gray caps are prescribed and the colored transversals are retained. The connector paths are $R_{1}$–$L_{1}$ and $R_{4}$–$L_{4}$–$L_{3}$–$R_{3}$–$R_{2}$–$L_{2}$, giving $γ = p^{−1}$. Numbering left $x$ and right $p(x)$ alike yields $R_{e}(e) = 1_{2}$. For a general corner element $z$, only the prescribed outer caps are deleted; every pair among retained ports, including caps, remains.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-corner-reduction"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:corner-reduction`)

An arrow labeled "glue" points to the middle panel. Middle panel labeled "connector in $e 1_4 e$": two columns of vertices labeled $R_1, R_2, R_3, R_4$ and $L_1, L_2, L_3, L_4$; a blue line connects $R_1$ to $L_1$; orange paths connect $R_2$ to $R_3$, $R_3$ to $L_3$, $L_3$ to $L_4$, $L_4$ to $R_4$, and $R_2$ to $L_2$; text below states "$\gamma(1) = 1$, $\gamma(4) = 2$". An arrow labeled "retain and renumber" points to the right panel. Right panel labeled "$R_e(e) = 1_2$": two columns of 2 vertices labeled 1, 2; horizontal edges connect left 1 to right 1 (blue) and left 2 to right 2 (green); text below states "$x$ and $p(x)$ share a number; right $4 = p(2)$ becomes 2".


Restoring the prescribed caps and the original indices reconstructs $z$, so $R_e$ is injective. Only nontransversal pairs were deleted, so it preserves rank, and by construction $R_e(e)=\mathbf 1_r$. To check multiplication, let $z,w\in e\mathcal D_{m}e$. The fixed caps at their middle interface are the right caps and left caps of $e$. Their connector is therefore the one just computed: the retained right port $p(x)$ of $z$ is connected to the retained left port $x$ of $w$. In the reduced numbering these ports have the same index. Contracting the connector, including when the reduced diagrams have caps of their own, thus gives ordinary multiplication in $\mathcal D_{r}$. It follows that $R_e(zw)=R_e(z)R_e(w)$, proving (i).

For (ii), suppose that $i\in X\cap Y$. The vertices $R_i,L_i$ in the connector have no incident caps, so their edge $R_iL_i$ is an entire connector path. Hence $\gamma(i)=i$, and $\gamma=p^{-1}$ implies $p(i)=i$. Thus every index in $X\cap Y$ is fixed by $e$, and $$\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(e)\subseteq([m]\setminus X)\cup([m]\setminus Y).$$ Both complements have size $m-r$, which proves the bound.

For (iii), consider the full glued graph $e\mathbf 1_m e$, keeping the middle identity diagram explicit. Its transversal paths have the same outside endpoint pairs as $e$, and so are indexed by the $r$ numbers used in the reduction. Mark a transversal path if it uses any middle identity edge whose index belongs to $J$. Let $J'$ be the numbers of the marked paths. A transversal path may use several middle identity edges; the only counting fact needed is that different paths are disjoint. Each middle identity edge therefore marks at most one transversal path, and $|J'|\leq|J|$.

Now replace the middle identity by any diagram $a$ supported in $J$. Every middle identity edge at an index outside $J$ remains present. An unmarked transversal path used only such edges, so that entire path survives with the same outside endpoints. Each of its internal vertices still has its two original incident edges; a new path cannot attach to it. In particular, changes to closed components or to paths with both endpoints on the same side cannot join an unmarked transversal: all its middle ports are already paired by the unchanged identity edges. Its corresponding reduced port is therefore still paired with the equally indexed port on the other side. Only numbers in $J'$ can belong to $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(R_e(eae))$. The construction of $J'$ did not depend on $a$, as required.

When $r=0$, the prescribed caps exhaust all outside ports. Then $eae=e$ for every $a$, and the corner is identified with the singleton $\mathcal D_{0}$. The reduction above is its unique map, and (iii) holds with $J'=\varnothing$; (ii) also holds since $|\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(e)|\leq m$. ◻

</details>

The reduction will also be applied to a submonoid $eSe$. We write $R_e(eSe)$ for its image in $\mathcal D_{r}$. It contains the ambient identity $\mathbf 1_r$. When discussing support in this reduced monoid, the port set is $[r]$; support in the original monoid still refers to $[m]$.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Homomorphic images and permutation lifts

Every element of a finite semigroup has an idempotent positive power. Indeed, for an element $x$ there are integers $a\geq1$ and $d\geq1$ such that $x^{k+d}=x^k$ for all $k\geq a$. Choose $N\geq a$ divisible by $d$. Then $x^{2N}=x^N$. If a multiplicative map sends $x$ to an idempotent $q$, the same is true of every positive power of $x$, including this idempotent power.

<a id="stmt-lem-unit-lifts"></a>
<div id="lem:unit-lifts" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Unit lifts from a minimum-rank corner). *Let $S\subseteq\mathcal D_{m}$ be nonempty and closed under multiplication, and let $\phi:S\to M$ be an onto multiplicative map to a monoid $M$. Let $q\in M$ be idempotent. There is an idempotent $e\in S$ with $\phi(e)=q$. For every such $e$, the reduced monoid $R_e(eSe)$ has a unital surjection $$\phi_e:R_e(eSe)\longrightarrow qMq,
  \qquad \phi_e(R_e(z))=\phi(z).$$ If $e$ has minimum rank among all idempotents in $S$ mapping to $q$, then every element of $eSe$ mapping to a unit of $qMq$ reduces to a permutation diagram. In particular, every unit of $qMq$ has a permutation lift in $R_e(eSe)$, and that lift is a unit of the reduced monoid.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-unit-lifts"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-unit-lifts</summary>

*Proof.* The sets $S$ and $M$ are finite because $\mathcal D_{m}$ is finite and $\phi$ is onto. Take any preimage of $q$ and then an idempotent positive power to obtain $e$. The image of $eSe$ under $\phi$ is exactly $qMq$: for any $v\in M$, a preimage $s\in S$ gives $\phi(ese)=qvq$. Its identity $e$ maps to the identity $q$ of this corner. Lemma <a href="#lem:corners" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:corners">4</a>(i) makes the displayed definition of $\phi_e$ well-defined and transfers both multiplication and surjectivity.

Suppose now that $e$ has the stated minimum rank, put $r=\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(e)$, and let $y\in eSe$ map to a unit $u$ of $qMq$. The group of units of $qMq$ is finite, so $u^t=q$ for some positive integer $t$. Take an idempotent positive power $z$ of $y^t$. Then $z\in S$ is idempotent and $\phi(z)=q$. The rank inequalities and minimum choice of $e$ give $$r\leq\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(z)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(y)\leq r.$$ The last inequality uses $y\in eSe$. Hence $R_e(y)$ has rank $r$ in $\mathcal D_{r}$ and is a permutation diagram. Its inverse is a power of the same finite-order permutation, so lies in $R_e(eSe)$. Surjectivity of $\phi_e$ supplies a lift for each unit $u$ and completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Corners of the relation monoid

For a finite set $H$ and a subset $F\subseteq H$, we regard a relation on $F$ as a relation on $H$ containing no pairs outside $F\times F$. With $i=I_{F}$, these are precisely the relations $A$ satisfying $A=iAi$. Under this identification their monoid is $i\mathcal R_{H}i$ with identity $i$. Permutations of a finite set are identified with their graph relations.

<a id="stmt-lem-relation-corner"></a>
<div id="lem:relation-corner" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (A relation corner). *Let $P\in\mathcal R_{H}$ be idempotent, and let $F\subseteq H$ satisfy $I_{F}PI_{F}=I_{F}$. Put $i=I_{F}$ and $Q=PiP$. Then $Q$ is idempotent, $PQ=QP=Q$, and restriction is a unital isomorphism $$\theta:Q\mathcal R_{H}Q\longrightarrow\mathcal R_{F},
  \qquad \theta(Z)=iZi.$$ Its inverse sends $A\in\mathcal R_{F}$ to $PAP=QAQ$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-relation-corner"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-relation-corner</summary>

*Proof.* The identities $P^2=P$ and $iPi=i$ give $$Q^2=PiP^2iP=P(iPi)P=Q,
  \qquad PQ=QP=Q.$$ They also give $iQ=iP$, $Qi=Pi$, and $iQi=i$. If $Z\in Q\mathcal R_{H}Q$, then $Z=QZQ$ and $PZP=Z$. Consequently $$Z=PiPZPiP=PiZiP.                         \tag{$*$}$$ Thus its restriction $iZi$ determines $Z$ uniquely.

Conversely, if $A\in\mathcal R_{F}$, so that $A=iAi$, then $$QAQ=QiAiQ=PiAiP=PAP,
  \qquad
  i(PAP)i=iPiAiPi=A.$$ Hence every relation on $F$ is a restriction of an element of the corner, and $A\mapsto PAP$ is the inverse bijection by $(*)$.

For multiplication, write $Z=PAP$ and $W=PBP$, where $A=iZi$ and $B=iWi$ are relations on $F$. Since $A=Ai$ and $B=iB$, we have $$ZW=PAP^2BP=PAPBP
     =PA(iPi)BP=PABP.$$ Restricting this equality gives $\theta(ZW)=AB=\theta(Z)\theta(W)$. Finally, the identity $Q$ of the source corner restricts to $i$, the identity of $\mathcal R_{F}$. This proves unitality and the claimed isomorphism. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:rank"></a>
### An exponential rank-loss bound

We now bound the rank lost by an idempotent diagram whose image adds one off-diagonal pair to the identity relation. The amplification step uses $32$ conjugates of that diagram to construct $256$ successive relation additions. Their joint support bounds the total rank loss, while a smaller relation corner bounds the loss at each addition.

For every integer $h\ge2$, set <a id="eq:rank-loss-function"></a>$$\label{eq:rank-loss-function}
 L(h)=2^{\lfloor(h-2)/31\rfloor}.$$ All reductions below are the maps $R_e$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:corners" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:corners">4</a>. Their ranges are regarded as submonoids of the indicated smaller matching monoids. In particular, their identities are the ambient identity diagrams, and they preserve absolute rank.

The theorem first assumes permutation lifts in addition to a unital surjection onto the *entire* relation monoid. A surjection onto only the relations generated by the displayed additions would not suffice: the proof must also lift arbitrary elements of smaller relation corners. Corollary <a href="#cor:divisor-degree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:divisor-degree">8</a> removes the extra lift assumption by passing to a minimum-rank idempotent corner.

<a id="stmt-thm-rank-loss"></a>
<div id="thm:rank-loss" class="theorem">

**Theorem 7** (Rank loss for a single relation addition). *Let $H$ be a finite set of size $h\ge2$, let $m\ge0$, and let $S\subseteq\mathcal D_{m}$ be a submonoid containing $\mathbf 1$. Suppose that $\phi:S\to\mathcal R_{H}$ is a unital surjection and that every permutation of $H$ has a lift in $S$ which is a permutation diagram. If $a\in S$ is idempotent and $$\phi(a)=I_{H}\cup\{(x,y)\},\qquad x,y\in H,\quad x\ne y,$$ then $$m-\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(a)\ge L(h).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-rank-loss"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-rank-loss</summary>

*Proof.* We induct on $h$, simultaneously for every ambient degree $m$ and every choice of $S,\phi,a$ satisfying the hypotheses. Put $t=16$. For $2\le h<2t+1=33$, one has $L(h)=1$. A full-rank diagram is a permutation, and an idempotent permutation is the identity. Since $\phi$ is unital and $\phi(a)\ne I_{H}$, the rank of $a$ is strictly smaller than $m$. This proves the base cases.

Now let $h\ge33$ and assume the theorem for every smaller size at least two. Write $$c=m-\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(a).$$

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
###### Conjugates with a common support bound.

<a id="eq:rank-conjugate-images"></a>
Choose pairwise disjoint sets $$U=\{u_1,\ldots,u_t\},\qquad
 V=\{v_1,\ldots,v_t\},\qquad \{z\}$$ in $H$. For each ordered pair of distinct points of $H$, a permutation $\pi$ can send $(x,y)$ to that pair. Choose a permutation-diagram lift $\widetilde\pi\in S$. Its inverse belongs to $S$ because a permutation diagram has finite order. In path order, $$\phi(\widetilde\pi^{-1}a\widetilde\pi)
 =\pi^{-1}\bigl(I_{H}\cup\{(x,y)\}\bigr)\pi
 =I_{H}\cup\{(\pi(x),\pi(y))\}.$$ Using the pairs $(u_i,z)$ and $(z,v_j)$ in this construction gives idempotents $s_i,s'_j\in S$ such that $$\label{eq:rank-conjugate-images}
 \phi(s_i)=I_{H}\cup\{(u_i,z)\},\qquad
 \phi(s'_j)=I_{H}\cup\{(z,v_j)\}
 \quad(1\le i,j\le t).$$ Conjugation by permutation diagrams preserves rank, so all these idempotents have rank $m-c$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:corners" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:corners">4</a>(ii), each has support of size at most $2c$. Thus the set $$J=\bigcup_{i=1}^{t}\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(s_i)
       \ \cup\ \bigcup_{j=1}^{t}\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(s'_j)
 \subseteq[m]$$ has size at most $4tc$. Every product $s_i s'_j$ is supported in $J$, because diagrams supported in a fixed set form a submonoid.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### A chain of $t^2$ additions.

<a id="eq:rank-generator-image"></a>
Put $P_0=I_{H\setminus\{z\}}$. Choose an idempotent $b_0\in S$ with $\phi(b_0)=P_0$: take any lift of $P_0$ and then a positive idempotent power. The image stays $P_0$ because $P_0$ is idempotent. For $1\le i,j\le t$, define $$g_{ij}=b_0s_i s'_j b_0\in b_0Sb_0.$$ In path order, the two added pairs in <a href="#eq:rank-conjugate-images" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-conjugate-images">[eq:rank-conjugate-images]</a> allow the path $u_i,z,v_j$. The outer factors $P_0$ delete pairs with endpoint $z$, so $$\label{eq:rank-generator-image}
 \phi(g_{ij})=P_0\cup\{(u_i,v_j)\}.$$ Figure <a href="#fig:amplification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:amplification">2</a> shows how the same $2t$ conjugates provide all $t^2$ choices of the pair $(u_i,v_j)$.

<figure id="fig:amplification">

<figcaption>The gray arrows are alternatives from the two families of relation images; each individual image has one added arrow. The highlighted path creates the selected pair after both outer factors delete endpoints at $z$. Identity loops are omitted. Only relations on $H$ are shown. The common matching support bound comes from taking the union of the supports of the $32$ conjugates and reducing at $b_{0}$; the same conjugates provide all $256$ choices.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-amplification"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:amplification`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,vertex/.style={circle,draw,minimum size=7mm,inner sep=1pt},every node/.style={font=\small}]
\node[vertex] (upperleft) at (0,1.2) {$u_1$};
\node[vertex] (middleleft) at (0,0) {$u_i$};
\node[vertex] (lowerleft) at (0,-1.2) {$u_t$};
\node at (0,0.6) {$\vdots$};
\node at (0,-0.6) {$\vdots$};
\node[vertex] (hub) at (2.2,0) {$z$};
\node[vertex] (upperright) at (4.4,1.2) {$v_1$};
\node[vertex] (middleright) at (4.4,0) {$v_j$};
\node[vertex] (lowerright) at (4.4,-1.2) {$v_t$};
\node at (4.4,0.6) {$\vdots$};
\node at (4.4,-0.6) {$\vdots$};
\draw[->,gray] (upperleft)--(hub);
\draw[->,very thick,blue!65!black] (middleleft)--node[above] {$\phi(s_i)$} (hub);
\draw[->,gray] (lowerleft)--(hub);
\draw[->,gray] (hub)--(upperright);
\draw[->,very thick,blue!65!black] (hub)--node[above] {$\phi(s'_j)$} (middleright);
\draw[->,gray] (hub)--(lowerright);
\node[align=center] at (1.05,1.7) {incoming\\family};
\node[align=center] at (3.35,1.7) {outgoing\\family};
\draw[->,thick] (5.15,0)--node[above,align=center] {sandwich\\by $P_0$} (6.65,0);
\node[vertex] (retainedleft) at (7.35,0) {$u_i$};
\node[vertex] (retainedright) at (9.2,0) {$v_j$};
\draw[->,very thick,blue!65!black] (retainedleft)--(retainedright);
\node[align=center] at (8.25,-0.9) {$\phi(g_{ij})=P_0\cup\{(u_i,v_j)\}$};
```
